mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i'd like to thank mrs palacio for her efforts on aviation safety. i agree with what she's said. i would also like to express my condolences to the families of the victims of the plane crash at sharm el-sheikh in egypt. i would then like to deal with the sensitive aspects of the discussions with the united states on aviation safety. the commission should be honoured for its fight to defend the integrity of the negotiations with the united states on the air passenger lists. some of us in the delegation for relations with the united states were in the united states discussing these issues. we know, in particular, how extremely difficult it is to get the united states to strike what we consider to be a reasonable and balanced balance between counter-terrorism and human rights. the negotiations have been partially successful and partially unsatisfactory. the united states has made some major concessions, however. the fact that information can be stored for three and a half years is positive from the american point of view. from a european perspective, however, this is hardly acceptable. however, in this case it is also about creating well-functioning links between the eu and the us so that industry, trade and air traffic can function and develop. in the light of this, it is urgent to find a solution that does not put an end to these contacts at a time when the economic situation is improving. i should warn you, however, of the contagious effects. three and a half years cannot be a template for the eu as we move forward with our own data protection and law enforcement legislation. in europe we have stronger protection of human rights than in the united states, especially in terms of privacy. we should be proud of that protection. as we in the eu develop our own counter-terrorism measures - in many cases similar but not identical to those taken by the us - we must defend our privacy protection. cooperation and coordination with the united states on these issues is necessary, but we cannot abandon our own principles of human rights. who wants to see armed guards and general requirements for fingerprints on european air traffic? the agreement must fall if the united states does not respect the protection of human rights. we cannot afford any room for loose interpretations on either side, either economically or humanly. the commission must have clear assurances on every point regarding interpretation and compliance. a broader international agreement on aviation safety could be a step in that direction.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr herzog, for a very, very extensive work and for many fruitful discussions, even if we have not been entirely in agreement. i would also like to thank the members of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, who have contributed a lot of discussion and many good amendments so that we have finally got a result from the committee on economic and monetary affairs which i think is very good. i am opposed to a general framework directive. in my view, this is not beneficial, but rather confusing. it forms an additional layer of legislation between the internal market competition rules and the sectoral directives and also hinders product development. the process of gradual deregulation of markets in the eu has been the most important component of improving services of general economic interest over the past decade. one of the reasons for deregulation was the generally poor quality of services before the market was opened. i am anxious that we do not have a monopoly situation again. i would therefore urge parliament to follow the conclusions of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy in the herzog report and not to vote in favour of the amendments tabled by the left. if we follow the position of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, we stimulate competition and product development and provide opportunities to offer consumers alternatives, both cheaper and better.
mr president, i would like to thank you. herzog has had a most ungrateful task. if there is one area where we are deeply divided in this house and in public opinion throughout the eu, it is precisely this area. the problem is that we have single market legislation which actually invites unlimited and unrestricted competition in both goods and services. this is something we cannot live with. it is said that this is mainly a matter of services of economic interest. what services are not of economic interest? is the education sector not of economic interest? isn't the healthcare sector of economic interest? are not all social services of great economic interest? it is all about welfare policy and we are talking about real political dynamism. if we want to balance development and stop this general attack on the welfare sectors, what should we do? should we go for a framework directive? i'm a little uncertain. a framework directive could in itself limit the scope of internal market legislation. it would be good to soften the climate and make the contradictions a little less violent. but in the political climate we have today and will have in the coming years, i fear that such a framework directive would be strongly dominated by market forces and right-wing forces. in that case, such a directive is a step in the wrong direction. then it could be a trap instead. is decentralisation a better option? is it better to invest in protecting local and regional activities? perhaps it would open up some other possibilities for a kind of protection and increased opportunities to wage a local struggle to preserve what we wish to preserve. above all, however, we would need time for research and discussion rather than the gigantic battle that we will face in the coming years.
in the case of amendment no 22, we believe that it is of the utmost importance to take action against environmental crime at sea. we welcome increased european cooperation between national coastguards in joint operations, planning and skills development. we therefore do not think it necessary to create a new parallel organization at european level. we look forward to the work of the european maritime safety authority.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) thousands of deliberate discharges of waste and cargo residues, including chemicals and oil, take place every year. the need for common legislation in this environmental field is therefore indisputable. on the other hand, it has not been demonstrated, much less proved, that the most appropriate and effective method of putting an end to these irregularities is to set up another european authority, a common european coast guard. better coordination between member states and increased accountability by national governments could prove equally effective. i therefore voted against amendments nos 6 and 22, which were nevertheless adopted (by 382 votes in favour, 82 votes against and 20 abstentions, and 392 votes in favour, 71 votes against and 33 abstentions, respectively).
in the case of the nuclear industry, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of nuclear installations is maintained. we believe that the swedish safety agency, together with the iaea, wenra and other existing organisations, complies satisfactorily with the requirements for the drawing up of safety regulations and the control of these. we have doubts, however, whether the eu has the necessary powers under the treaty to decide on a nuclear safety directive, at least as regards the seppänen report. we have chosen to vote in favour of the report in the final vote because it has not been possible to vote on the proposed legal basis for the directive and because the report is clearly much better than the commission's proposal.
in the case of the nuclear industry, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of nuclear installations is not compromised. i also voted in favour of amendment no 30 by the green group, which seeks to remove obstacles to fairer competition between nuclear and other energy sources on the energy market.
in writing. - (sv) i voted in favour of amendment no 19 by the green group (which was rejected by 71 votes to 235 with 11 abstentions). this amendment emphasizes that inhabitants of potentially affected areas in neighbouring countries should have the same rights as citizens of the country where the plant is located, for example, in public consultations when granting loans from the community budget. it is important that the espoo convention is respected in practice.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner nielson, this is the police. of course, it was with great shock that we learned the terrible news of the earthquake in bam. in the middle of the christmas weekend, television screens were filled with horrific images of injured and disaster-stricken people, thousands desperately searching for their loved ones in a city in ruins. on behalf of the eldr group, i would like to express our deep sympathy and condolences to the iranian people at this difficult time. of course, the eu should continue to work with the iranian authorities to help provide the victims with care, shelter and other necessities. we should also help rebuild this beautiful historic city. our concern for and solidarity with the iranian people also extends to their access to democracy and human rights. the eldr group is very concerned. there is still a lot to be done, because the iranian regime is anything but democratic. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment. we are extremely concerned about the latest developments and the guardian council's interference in the upcoming election. by rejecting thousands of candidates across the country - more than half of those who applied - the guardian council of iran is once again demonstrating its unwillingness to reform the iranian government. it sounds hollow to classify the underdogs as a bunch of criminals on the grounds that they have been in contact with, among other things, a number of groups that the guardian council dislikes. by that logic, a quarter of the legislators already in office would be criminals. if iran ever wants to be counted among the democratic states, it must accept that political legitimacy is derived from the people in free elections, not by the arbitrary interpretation of the ayatollahs of what is the will of god. the iranian people, like everyone else, want democracy and human rights. we in the eldr group would like to call on the iranian leaders not to interfere in the elections but to allow the candidates to stand so that they can be as fair and open as possible. in this context, i would like to join my colleagues in calling on the commission to strengthen and develop the human rights dialogue with iran. it's got to be more than just polite coffee talk. for it to be meaningful, it must be clear, persistent and consistent, and it must be regularly evaluated. i hope that you, commissioner nielson, together with chris patten and javier solana, can act to bring fresh blood into these talks, also in the light of the work to get iran to ratify and implement the non-proliferation treaty.
- the next item is the commission communication on services in the internal market. after the commissioner's speech, we will use the catch-the-eye procedure.
commission. madam president, i assume that you want me to say something – i used to speak your language, but i have not done so for about 40 years! i have come to the house this evening to make two declarations: the first is on the services directive, which the commission decided upon this afternoon; the other is a short announcement on behalf of the commission, and in particular of my colleague mr solbes, on the strategy for economic policy coordination and surveillance. i shall start with the services directive. it is with great pleasure that i can announce to you today that the commission has adopted a proposal for a directive to create a real internal market in services. it is an ambitious but balanced and gradual proposal, which aims to set service businesses free of legal and administrative barriers to the development of cross-border activities. it is potentially the biggest boost to the internal market since its launch in 1993. it will improve the competitiveness not only of the services sector but also of the whole european economy, including the manufacturing sector. the proposal is a major element in getting the lisbon agenda back on track. it is the result of a long process launched by the commission's services strategy in december 2000. it addresses the internal market barriers identified in the commission’s report on the state of the internal market in services in july 2002. the european parliament was involved throughout the process and adopted supportive opinions on both these documents. i am therefore delighted that i can come to you first and present our proposed solution today. the proposal responds to the european parliament's call for a comprehensive proposal to ensure free movement of services by means of applying the country of origin principle, administrative cooperation and, where strictly necessary, harmonisation. the proposed directive is comprehensive in that it covers all the services that are at present unable to benefit from the internal market because of legal or administrative barriers. it covers both the freedom of establishment and freedom to provide services. it will benefit a wide variety of services, including professional services, business services, security services, environmental services, distribution services, travel agencies, hotels, restaurants and entertainment, as well as health services. only those services that are already covered by wide-ranging specific eu law, such as financial services, telecommunications and transport, are excluded. in order to make it easier to establish a service business in another member state, the proposal will cut red tape through a large-scale administrative simplification process. a number of restrictions on establishment, such as nationality requirements or 'economic needs tests', will be abolished. in addition, member states will be obliged to set up electronic procedures and make available to service providers single points of contact dealing with authorisations and other formalities. unnecessary authorisation procedures will be screened and removed; remaining authorisation schemes will be made more transparent and predictable, being based exclusively on objective criteria known in advance. other restrictions, such as requirements that limit the number of outlets per head of population, or fixed tariffs, will also undergo a transparent process of screening and mutual evaluation. in order to make it easier to provide services across borders, the proposed directive aims to implement in practice the country of origin principle, whereby once a service provider is operating legally in one member state, it can provide its services in others without having to comply with further rules in those 'host' member states. in order to underpin this principle, the proposal provides for enhanced administrative cooperation between member states and for some basic, harmonised, eu-wide quality requirements. it will, for instance, require appropriate professional indemnity insurance for services giving rise to particular risks. the proposed directive recognises the specific nature of the regulated professions and the particular role of self-regulation and calls on professional associations to establish european codes of conduct on professional ethics. it is complementary to and fully compatible with the proposed directive on the recognition of professional qualifications. in order to make it possible to use services offered in other member states, the proposal also clarifies and strengthens the rights of users of services. it therefore makes it clear that those rights prevent member states from imposing restrictions such as requiring authorisation to use services (for example of architects or builders from other member states), or discriminatory tax rules making services from other eu countries more costly. the proposed directive clarifies the rights of patients by setting out, based on the case-law of the european court of justice, the conditions under which national social security systems must reimburse the costs of medical care received in other member states. the proposed directive seeks to create more cross-border service activity, which will give businesses new opportunities in new markets, so that they can grow and create more jobs. increased competition will also be likely to reduce prices and help to disseminate best practice and encourage innovation. in particular, smes will greatly benefit from the improved legal certainty and increased opportunities for cross-border supply. the effect on the economy as a whole will be higher competitiveness and more and better jobs. therefore, the proposal needs to be adopted, written into national law and implemented as quickly as possible. this means that it must get the highest political support from all the institutions involved. the commission is aware that, unfortunately, this proposal arrives too late to achieve first reading in this parliament. however, given that it meets the european parliament's request for a comprehensive approach to create a true internal market in services, i trust that members will give it due consideration and start to discuss it in order to provide a sound basis for the new parliament's work and show the way to the council of ministers. that is what i would like to say on the directive on services. now, very briefly, on the other subject, i should like to say that the commission has decided today to challenge the council conclusions in the european court of justice. this challenge will, however, concentrate exclusively on procedural elements and will not touch on the country-specific economic surveillance aspects of the council conclusions. more details are available in a document literally just made public, with the reference number ip/04/35.
- thank you very much, lieutenant bolkestein. we would be particularly grateful if you would inform us of the result immediately.
commission. madam president, i thank the members that have put questions to the commission. first, i should like to deal with the subject of the services directive. i will then move on to the other subject. i thank mr harbour and mrs villiers for their support for the directive, or at least for what i said about that directive in general terms. mr harbour said quite frankly that approval by parliament is all very well, but what about the other half of the legislative body in the union – the council? he is quite right in saying that the council – not only on the issue that he mentioned, but others as well – has been laggardly. that is very bad. sales promotion, the example quoted by mr harbour, is only one example: there are others. mr harbour asked for the council to accept a serious timetable. i wish i could achieve this. as mr harbour knows, the commission is very powerful, but it is not powerful enough to force the council to stick to a timetable. we are in the hands of the presidency. mr harbour can count on the commission to impress upon the irish presidency that it should do some serious work on this. however, when all is said and done, the presidency presides and the commission plays the role of the greek tragedy chorus: it looks on, but there is not much it can do but say 'woe!'. nevertheless, we will chivvy the irish presidency along, and tell it to get on with the job. mrs villiers complained that financial services are not included. the reason for that is that we have a very elaborate framework for financial services, as mrs villiers knows better than almost anyone else. the financial services action plan, with its 42 measures, is now well on its way to completion. we did not want to muddy the water or go over the same ground again, and that is why, as with some other fields that are regulated by other legal instruments, we thought it better to exclude financial services. with regard to the stability and growth pact, and the fact that the commission has decided to challenge the decision of the council in the court of justice, mr mombaur said that the commission must insist on budgetary discipline. the commission certainly does that: that is why we are going to court. i am pleased that on the whole – if not across the board – parliament supports the commission in that endeavour. the commission believes that we live in a community of law. the stability pact is an agreement between parties and an agreement between parties is the law between parties, and that law must be upheld. i said earlier that the proceeding the commission would like to start in the court of justice will concern procedural aspects. we do not think it is the proper role of the european court of justice to tell the union how to formulate economic policy. therefore, the court will focus on procedural aspects – the areas where it believes that the ecofin council has gone astray. having said that, i should like to point out that procedure and substance form a whole. it is for the court of justice to decide where the member states – or the council – have gone astray. i hope that will happen soon. we do not want this procedure to drag on for years. we hope that a fast-track procedure can be followed so that we will receive the opinion of the court in the autumn. mrs villiers repeated her well-known song in pointing out that she is against the euro or, at any rate, that britain should not join emu. i will not enter into a debate on that now. i know that fools rush in where angels fear to tread, so i will not get mixed up in the uk debate on the euro. she also said that 'inevitably' – that is the word she used – these problems arise when there are 12 countries without one over-arching economic policy that is followed by all 12. i beg to differ. twelve countries have signed up to the stability and growth pact. if, and as long as, they do what they signed up to, there is no problem about 12 countries following different economic policies. as long as their budget deficits are below the famous barrier of 3%, i do not see what more we need. no doubt the commission will come up with more proposals along those lines. that is my answer to mrs villiers on the point of the link between sovereignty in economic affairs and deficits in fiscal policy.
- thank you, commissioner bolkestein. - i'll see you in a few. this meeting is closed.
- the next item is the recommendation for second reading (a5-0455/2003) by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy on the common position of the council with a view to the adoption of a regulation of the european parliament and of the council on detergents (10595/3/2003 - c5-0521/2003 - 2002/0216 (cod)) (rapporteur: mr nobilia).
madam president, i would like to thank you. our group has chosen to support the compromise amendments tabled by the rapporteur. we would have liked to go further in several areas and have stricter legislation, but we think it is very important that we take a decision and that this comes into force as soon as possible. the possibility for individual member states to maintain and introduce stricter rules in this area, as several member states have already done, are proposals which are particularly important to us. one of the main issues is the abolition of phosphates. there are good reasons to end that use immediately. we know that the phosphates lead to a lot of over-fertilization. in the marine environment, for example, they create major environmental problems. we know that there are adequate substitutes and that countries have in practice phased out the use of phosphates. sometimes in politics there is a contradiction between what is politically possible and what is desirable. we have therefore chosen to make a compromise which is politically possible, i.e. phasing out phosphates over three years. it would be desirable to phase them out today. we'll try to go both ways. we will vote for the green amendments to phase them out immediately, but we believe that it is the political compromise that will be adopted in the end.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur and all the parties involved on the proposal that has been put forward. i think they have done a surprisingly good job. it is an important report for the environment but also a major and important consumer issue, because detergents make up a large part of what we use every day. it is necessary to provide consumers with complete and sufficient information to enable them to guide market developments by choosing environmentally friendly products. as regards phosphates, the argument in sweden, where we still have some detergents containing phosphates, is that we have both treatment plants that handle the phosphates and that the alternatives are not entirely problem-free. i have accepted these arguments. in amendments nos 40 to 46, which the green group is now tabled, we note that the scientific committee has said that phosphates are a major problem and that the alternatives available do not cause these problems. that said, i think it is time to ban them altogether if they are so dangerous, if they are destroying the mediterranean, etc. let's not wait for that. i would therefore like us to vote in favour of these amendments so that the commission can make a proposal on this.
commission. madam president, i thank members of parliament for this very constructive debate. i want to come back to the issue of phosphates, which was raised by a number of members. we must remember that fertilisers are a very important source of phosphates: about 50% come from fertilisers. years ago, in the country which i know well, i proposed taxation on fertilisers high in phosphates. that solved the problem to a certain extent. in many countries the authorities have acted. the use of phosphates in detergents is effectively banned by means of national legislation or voluntary agreements in member states that have deemed such measures necessary. national measures currently exist in the majority of member states and, in some cases, they have been in place for many years. that is why the commission will now concentrate on ensuring that any proposal in this area has a secure scientific basis and that it has an added value at community level. the scientific committee found that the consultants' report on phosphates was seriously lacking in some respects – for example concerning the impact of alternatives – and it is has identified a number of other areas for which data is still lacking. i want to assure you that the commission is fully committed to filling these data gaps as rapidly as possible and to proposing whatever measures may be justified on that basis.
- thank you, lieutenant liikanen. this meeting is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon.
- the next item is the report (a5-0474/2003) by mr sandbæk, on behalf of the committee on development and cooperation, on the communication from the commission updating the community action programme: rapid action against hiv/aids, malaria and tuberculosis in the context of poverty reduction. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas:
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is the moral duty of the european union and the individual member states to make every effort to combat aids, malaria and tuberculosis. 20,000 deaths a day, or more than 7 million deaths a year, are horrendous figures. we can think of the plague that raged here in europe in the middle ages. the black plague is back in our world. 41 percent of the world's population is at risk of contracting malaria. in the light of the un millennium development goals and the g8 summit in evian on sustainable development, i would like to say to mr poul nielson that no area should be spared in terms of providing for people affected by malaria, tuberculosis and hiv/aids and in terms of prevention. it is really about the rearmament of the entire civilization of this earth. women must no longer be exploited, sexually degraded and subjected to a slavery which is totally unworthy of a modern and civilized society. we need a new dignity on the part of men towards women all over the world. a society with a new and strengthened way of life is needed, where men show respect for women and for the dignity of the human person. i would urge commissioner nielson to get involved in lifestyle issues, not least in relation to the male lifestyle which in this way, not least in relation to the spread of hiv/aids, degrades women and contributes to a completely immoral spread.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i think the commission's update report was very good. i also think that sandbaek has made a very good report. she has put forward a number of proposals which strengthen the areas where the commission has indicated that strengthening is needed to achieve the objectives of poverty reduction and the fight against these diseases. i would like to take up paragraph 26 of the sandbaek report, which is very important. it reminds us that the annual repayment of debts and interest amounts to almost 40 percent of the gdp of the least developed countries. this is tremendous; i think the eu should take the initiative to write off its debt. it could also be possible to allocate such a debt write-off to be used primarily for hiv/aids, malaria and tuberculosis and perhaps also for education. this would indeed speed up and strengthen the fight against poverty.
commission. madam president, mrs sandbæk referred to the unspent edf money that could be used for this. the amount of commitments not yet spent on the budget part of our development cooperation is eur 11 billion. for the edf it is eur 8 billion. we are now at a point where it takes the commission and the member states pretty much the same number of years to implement things. today we are not slower, but we have a mountain of old decisions to implement. this is true for both budget and edf areas. at the end of 2003 we found that we had reached a level of edf spending of around eur 2.5 billion: the highest ever, around 60% higher than the level when this commission took office. it was announced to member states that they had better be prepared to pay up for the last quarter of that year so that we could deliver on this better performance. unfortunately, however, some member states did not pay their contributions, so we had to ask them to make up their minds as to whether they were serious about their wish for the commission to perform better. the commission is serious about that. the trips agreement is being addressed by the commission. we were pleased that it was in all, the total amount pledged to the fund is eur 4.8 billion. i agree with those who said that this is still not enough money, but it should be considered that our budget support for the health - the next item is the report (a5-0329/2003) by mr bébéar, on behalf of the committee on development and cooperation, on the new partnership for africa's development (nepad) (2003/2106 (ini)).
commission. madam president, i shall start by commenting on mrs kinnock's remark about the need to mobilise similar support for other things as we do for the peace facility. we do not spend similar amounts of money on all the other things: we spend much more. what is new about the peace facility is that we are creating a basis for spending money on things we were never able to spend money on before. funding the implementation of the peace operations decided by africa and implemented in an african context is a new thing. we should make sure that we only use that money for things that cannot be funded through the regular systems already in place. i liked mr corrie's remark about oil-rich african states which could do more. we should come back to this. i also see that it would be very meaningful in the joint parliamentary assembly to discuss the performance of oil-exporting economies and other similar extraction industries in africa. this discussion is just starting up, but that is in many cases where the big money should be found for social purposes. the fantastic things about nepad were its timing and character, and the ambition of accepting exposure and measurement as regards the global criteria relating to democracy. these african countries are saying that they want to be judged on these same principles, echoing what we had all agreed on in the cotonou framework. they wanted this to be taken up. they did it up against the g8, which attracted much attention globally and, in that sense, raised the stakes in the process of democratisation in africa. nepad, and democracy in africa, are still very new. the best performers have had only two – at the most three – reasonably fair elections. we tend to forget that: the best performers have experienced two acceptable democratic elections. there is not much more than a big handful of those best cases. however, things are moving in the right direction. this peer review is a fantastic, very daring endeavour. of course they had to institutionalise. it was a wise move to set it into the context of african union as soon as possible, because, as mr van orden correctly pointed out, what would they have done with zimbabwe and a large number of other cases if the original five heroes of nepad had handled that? they would have run out of political authority. it would have been perceived as a kind of ganging-up – showing the others how to do things. instead, it was wisely embedded – if i may use that word with reference to that continent – into the african union, which took it up. this work is continuing. it is quite fantastic that they are lining up standards to get through this screening in the peer review process. i told them in one discussion that it looks as if it is more difficult to get good marks in the peer review process than it is to fulfil the criteria we have defined in order to become a member of the european union. it is very ambitious. i can only recommend that everybody interested in africa studies how they are setting the mark there. i have warned them that it may be too ambitious, but they want this to be a global standard for governance. they know it is not going to be easy. institutionally, we look forward very much to a systematic, close, well-organised collaboration with the emerging commission in addis ababa. i hope that we will get back on track and be able to manage the difficulties – also with reference to zimbabwe. i hope that 2004 will be a positive turning-point, enabling us at full speed to resume the eu-africa dialogue.
- thank you, chief nielson. this meeting is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon.
- the next item is the report (a5-0477/2003) by mr prets, on behalf of the committee on culture, youth, education, the media and sport, on the preservation of cultural diversity: the role of the european regions and international organisations (e.g. unesco and the council of europe) (2002/2269 ((ini)).
commission - madam president, you will understand that, because there are no fighters in the chamber, i am not going to answer all those who, as i understand very well, left the chamber at a quarter past midnight. but let me say a few words that are very dear to my heart. it is the luxembourgish woman who is speaking to you, and it is the luxembourgish woman who has developed with you the european year of languages, of all languages, without exception: recognised languages, unrecognised languages, majority languages, minority languages, because i have always said that there are no major languages, there are no minor languages, there are only mother tongues, and they are all major by definition. so, let's continue in that direction. we have developed our language action plan based on this multilingualism, and let me tell you that multilingualism includes our non-official languages, but certainly not esperanto, because we have enough living languages that are struggling to create, alongside that, artificial languages. so let us teach our children languages and, in this regard, i am still glad to see that almost all - and i would even say all - of our ministers of education are making an effort, and some are starting from scratch. they make an effort to introduce languages into primary education. there's been a huge improvement in the last two years. we are still far from the end, but we are making progress and at least the ministers who are not making an effort have a bad conscience. and it is up to you, members of parliament, to continue to give them a bad conscience so that reforms at local, regional and national level can be implemented. the commissioner also tells every minister he meets that maintaining multilingualism in europe is a matter of survival. i would now like to answer some specific questions, even though the members are no longer in the chamber. as regards unesco and our allies, it is clear that at unesco we need all countries to form a majority in order to adopt a convention on cultural diversity, and we need to set the example of cultural diversity at the global level. so what we are trying to do, and we are succeeding in doing, by setting an example and insisting on respect for minorities, is to change mindsets, especially in countries - and some have been mentioned - that are very far from perfect, and we all know that. you parliamentarians know this, because you are constantly discussing this, but it is by taking these minorities with us and telling them that we do not accept that they are left behind that we can help them, not by building a wall between those who are right and those who are wrong. i would like to give you a very concrete example. for the first time last may we opened the cannes film festival with european cinema day. twenty-five ministers, proud of their national film production, accompanied by young and well-known directors, showed the world the importance they attached to cinema. and on the afternoon of this european cinema day, we organised, with our partners worldwide, a conference on world cinema to give a voice to those who have not and to give a chance to the cinema of those minorities who, without europe's help, would never have had the opportunity to express themselves. that must be the european policy. it is a policy that sets an example for us and presents our unique model. i now turn to mr beazley, who asked me a very specific question, asking whether we would be drawing up a communication on unesco. mr beazley, i do not know if you have noticed, but in august the commission published, for the first time in the history of the european union, i believe, a communication on unesco, which was an instrument to move things forward. now we've won a battle. i say a battle, because it's not over. the groups hope - the first one has started to meet - that there will now be discussions at unesco level. i believe that at the end of 2004 or the beginning of 2005 the international negotiations in the strict sense of the term will be able to begin. it is only then, when we have a starting text, that we can define what europe is seeking to achieve in these negotiations. it is not enough to present a communication every three months which would be of no use. i am a believer in realpolitik, as the germans say: i want to take action that produces results. our communication last summer helped to get things started and to show that europe was involved in the development of an international instrument. now, as soon as the discussion has produced the first results, we will state the directions we wish to give to the debate. so let's be effective and not activism for the sake of activism. i regret to have to tell mrs junker that, as regards the concentration of the media, we may have the same idea at the outset, but we are also based on the same treaty. however, it prevents the commission from presenting an instrument for harmonising and preserving the diversity and pluralism of the media. the treaty being what it is and the commission being the guardian of the treaties, i cannot do better. i will never get the commission's approval if i depart from the treaty. we can only hope, madam president, that the new treaty, as several members have clearly stated, will contain much stronger elements concerning this diversity in all its aspects, because it is multifaceted. it concerns not only dance and the arts, but also and above all language, minorities, cinema, and the pluralism of expressions, and therefore of the media and the audiovisual sector. i would therefore like the new treaty to give more scope to the representatives of the people first of all, but also to the commission's initiative to take it a step further. it is said that the world is not a commodity, much less culture. in this connection, i believe that the commission has made it very clear in its action on external trade, but also on the authorisation of national aid, for example, for public and other cinema and television, that it is not prepared to accept that culture is considered a commodity. but even if it is not a commodity, culture has a price and must be paid. so it is up to us to convince national governments and to ensure that europe can invest enough to keep these cultures alive. europe must have the opportunity to be a living example to the world, to change the world, because even if our experience of diversity and respect for others is not perfect, our example is still interesting. let's lead the way, and the world will be a better place.

occupied territories, which also applies to eu citizens, aid workers, journalists and others. we expect the eu to protest. however, as regards the environment, which is my area of expertise, i think task for the eu in the next six months, when the kyoto protocol threatens to collapse. other major issues are the cartagena protocol and the kuala lumpur biodiversity conference, where important decisions will be taken on trade in genetically engineered products. this is the old eu's baby, and it should be protected. another chapter is also missing in this programme, which is otherwise good. the commission decided to bring an action before the court of justice against the council's decision. the crisis is that rules have been set which do not correspond to reality. when reality and the rules collide, this political crisis arises and the solution can only be one: to reconsider the stability pact as it stands today.

in writing. - (sv) we fully support the recognition of cultural diversity as a fundamental right and we therefore choose to support the prets report on cultural diversity. the eu is and must remain a mosaic of minorities and cultures. as liberals, we always put the individual at the centre of all political decisions. we therefore believe that it is of the utmost importance for international law to confirm that each state or group of states has a legal right to freely determine the cultural policy it wishes to pursue and to strengthen the policy of international cooperation and solidarity in cultural matters. linguistic diversity must also be preserved within the eu. we are, however, skeptical about the idea that the eu should have a legal right to adopt measures in the field of culture and the media to preserve and promote cultural diversity. we therefore do not support the eu developing a legal instrument for cultural diversity.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating both the commission and the rapporteur on a very excellent report on a pressing subject. there is no doubt that in our after the war and grew up in a residential area where the inhabitants were mainly from estonia, latvia, lithuania, and finland. about half my neighbors came as immigrants from other countries. they have contributed to swedish welfare just like everyone else. we would not have the prosperity we have today if these people, along with the rest of us, had not contributed to that prosperity. we're in a different place today. we have a refugee policy and an immigration policy. we must build a common refugee policy based on international conventions. europe must be a refuge for people who are persecuted and at risk of death in other countries. we must have a humane refugee policy in which we take on a shared responsibility. immigration policy is another matter. it is about people who are not in danger of being killed in their own countries, but who would like to come to europe, for their own sake, to have a better life, but also to contribute to our societies, which they do. they are already doing so today, and the need will be greater in the future. we know the demographic situation and we need growth, employment and prosperity in europe. we need to increase employment in the future and we need people for that employment, to be able to cope with elderly care and health care. we need people, also from other countries, along with those who are already here. unfortunately, people coming from other countries are becoming largely unemployed today, and we should of course do something about that. i am convinced that we need an immigration policy. immigrants must be integrated as soon as possible. integration policy is important and employment comes with education. this applies in particular to women, which dybkjær was keen to do. this is important for the sake of women themselves, but also for the integration of the next generation into society. let me also say something about the issue which has created a little bit of turbulence, namely the right to vote. i have experience from the euro vote in sweden. we applied the same rules as in local elections, that is, people from other countries were allowed to vote. they went out and voted very heavily. if they had been allowed to decide the outcome of the swedish vote, sweden would today have the euro as its currency. they were more supportive, they were more european than the other swedes. think about it.

the rapporteur's alternate. mr president, i would like to thank you. the european union is delivering. the efficiency of the codecision procedure makes a substantial contribution here. the citizens' demands for more growth, more jobs and development are being met. the public procurement conciliation which we are now debating is an expression of this. modernising and opening up europe's public procurement market is necessary to increase europe's competitiveness. the public procurement directive stipulates full competition between tenderers. full transparency and equal treatment are guaranteed. this benefits both europe's taxpayers, who get better value for their money, and the countless companies that now have the opportunity to compete for contracts across the union. from the parliament's point of view, the settlement was a success. few of us had previously thought that the final result would be so close to our starting position. the administration of the procurement will be considerably simplified. the directive will be simpler and clearer than its predecessors. the user-friendliness is enhanced not least by the electronic trading platforms being established, which simplifies for smes with limited administrative resources. the dialogue between contracting authorities and suppliers is improved and incorporated into the directive. our aim was to increase competitiveness, strengthen equal treatment and improve our tools against organised crime and corruption. it was necessary to increase transparency, not least in terms of who was awarded a contract and why. it was these issues and the possibility of including social and environmental considerations that forced the settlement. the directive prevents the arbitrary or unbalanced consideration of social and environmental aspects, but such considerations can nevertheless be taken into account. the court of justice's jurisdiction has helped us to achieve balance, its case law has been implemented and this provides legal certainty in this area. for this encouraging result i would like to thank in particular the rapporteur, mr stefano zappalà, but also the shadow rapporteur, mrs maria berger, who has made a great effort, the whole of parliament's delegation, but also the two commissioners present today, who have really not been sitting on the sidelines, to put it simply by swedish standards, but have really made a great tangible contribution. i would even like to thank the presidency, which dealt with this matter and with whom we negotiated, which is unusual for this parliament. without all these efforts, this step would have been impossible. we could not have come this far if there had not been a concrete political will on the part of all parties to deliver for europe in this area. that is why we were able to reach an agreement despite some rather difficult political differences at the outset. we expect this step to contribute to a more intensive european trade exchange with the resulting prosperity gains for the union, for our citizens and for our european populations. thank you to everyone who contributed!
mr president, i would like to thank you. we have worked long on these important procurement directives and it is now time for a final debate and a vote on the outcome of the conciliation. i would like to take this opportunity to give a brief overview. the commission's original proposal was, from a green point of view, totally unacceptable in terms of environmental and social criteria for public procurement. however, i was very optimistic about the procurement directives after a majority in the european parliament, both at first and second reading, strongly supported the proposal to give contracting authorities greater freedom and legal rights to take account of and incorporate environmental and social criteria in public procurement. unfortunately, i felt from the very beginning that the commission did not really want to acknowledge the fact that it is clear from articles 2 and 6 of the treaty, as well as from the sixth environmental action programme and other policy documents, that environmental and social considerations must be integrated into all eu policies and that public procurement must be used to achieve the goal of sustainable development. the result of the compromise is clearly better than the commission's original proposal and the old rules, but unfortunately i think we have missed a unique opportunity to be as progressive as the promises and the talk of sustainable development would require. i think the council has shown greater willingness and flexibility, for example by raising the threshold. however, i believe that the conciliation process failed to resolve the important question of whether public procurement worth 14% of gdp could really be a difference in eu environmental policy and promote 'green' procurement. i thank my colleagues who fought hard for this during the conciliation negotiations. the so-called environmental characteristics criteria are among the criteria which, according to the agreed text, may be set by the contracting entity. however, this term does not include the production methods for the production of a good, nor the life cycle perspective, i.e. the period from the production of the good to waste, nor the long-term effects or internationalisation of the external costs. what is meant by environmental criteria? is it not true that there will be a debate on the interpretation of what this means and, with it, legal uncertainty? i think we have missed a brilliant opportunity to promote sustainable development and an improved quality of life for this and future generations. this was also the reason why the representatives of the greens/efa group voted against the final vote of the conciliation committee. i think that many of the members of the verts/ale group will vote in favour of the proposal - at least i will. despite this criticism, i naturally hope that the legislative package will promote open and free procurement and also ultimately promote environmental and social considerations in this context.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very pleased to see that we are finally getting a comprehensive set of rules within the union on public procurement. i am naturally particularly pleased that this framework allows the contracting authority to use criteria other than price. i think it is important that we use public procurement to also influence social developments. we shall favour products and producers who act in a social direction which we advocate. environmental issues are central. if we want to reduce pollution in food and the external environment, we must favour producers who are willing to do so. if we want equality in working life, we must favour workplaces that have that focus. if we want all people to have the opportunity to work and to be self-supporting, we must of course favour companies which create jobs for people with disabilities. if we want to create a society that provides greater opportunities for participation and justice, we must ensure that society is designed in such a way. it is worth asking whether we could have gone further in the conciliation process. this is a question that you always ask yourself as a negotiator. having spent most of my adult life negotiating, i know that in negotiation, you have to reach a point where the parties agree. when you have reached that point, as i think we did in the negotiations, you have to say that you are ready to present your results. i think the result we have presented in our decision is something we can stand up for and actually be proud of. parliament's position on public procurement shows that decisions at community level can be important in achieving a more humane society. it is a positive decision after a long and sometimes arduous work. i want to thank everyone who has contributed to this result.

mr president, i would like to thank you. it is about a hundred years since the labor movement in europe demanded legal working hours. eight hours of work, eight hours of leisure, eight hours of sleep were required at that time. eventually, that program was implemented. this was important in many ways, because it meant that companies were not competing with work hours. you had to compete in other areas instead. it also meant protecting workers and giving society a fixed framework for work, schooling, leisure and other activities. i am really shocked that the ppe-de group and several other political groups seem to want to break with this tradition and instead go back to the situation that existed a hundred years ago, when there was voluntary participation in the labour market. i note that there is no mention of voluntary agreements between trade unions and employers' organizations. instead, it is a matter of voluntary agreements between individual workers and individual employers. it's a regression that is incomprehensible. i do not understand how one can argue that way or suggest such a thing. it is deeply irresponsible and will lead to strife and conflict in the labour markets. it will result in people in such a competitive labour market being forced to take jobs on the terms that apply. you can call it voluntary or involuntary, but in a competitive labour market you have to assume that you have the market value you have. you take what you're given. that's the thing about volunteering. this affects family life too, as many have said. parents are forced to work at times that make it difficult to balance work and family life and to take children into account. i would also add that it affects all of us as consumers. it has not been emphasized much, but we risk injury when we are operated on by exhausted surgeons, we can be run over by half-asleep truck drivers, and so on. we're all victims of work-hunting and stress. it is much better for all parties if we can agree to stop these derogations which the united kingdom got when the directive was adopted. they were in fact in breach of the rule of equal competition. now employers in other countries are saying 'if the uk can have an exemption, we want an exemption too'. this is the exception that becomes the rule and we cannot live with that. so we have to stop and go the other way. we must remove the uk's exemption and find other ways of solving these flexibility problems. we cannot do without a general labour law.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur for a very good report. sometimes when i listen to the speeches i get the feeling that the health and safety aspects for the individual are not the main point, which they really are for legislation. i am frankly frightened when i hear the british representatives of the ppe-de group and the eldr group. they say that, given that some jobs have such low wages, we must let the wage earners work more to compensate for these low wages. what is it but the model found in the united states, where one sometimes needs two or three jobs to provide for one's family at all? is this what we have agreed in the lisbon process? we must have competitiveness and growth, but we must also have high social standards and the possibility of reconciling family life and work. these principles, which we have spoken about, are completely contrary to that kind of flexibility. the problem is that the exception has become the rule in the united kingdom. it is never meant that an exception should become the rule, but there it has become so. if the derogation is extended, there is a risk that member states will compete with longer working hours to attract production. is this the kind of europe we want, where we compete socially? no, i do not want such a europe. however, i think we should have flexibility, as thorning-schmidt said. however, i do not see the individual exemption as an option. consider the dependence of the individual employee on the company in negotiating pay and hours. it's not voluntary, even if you say it is. it is much better to use the social partners. there are differences between sectors and sometimes special arrangements may be needed for certain sectors to achieve flexibility while maintaining the principles of health and safety. i therefore advocate collective bargaining, where this flexibility can be achieved. i am not in favour of renationalisation, because it has the same effect. it would mean that countries would compete with each other by attracting capital because health and safety are not priorities. we want our european model to stimulate competitiveness, but at the same time to have high social objectives and the possibility of reconciling work and parenting. let's live up to that model. we must then remove the individual exemption, while maintaining flexibility and a high level of security.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the commission's communication on the reform of state-owned enterprises in developing countries. i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to present this report. the high standard of living and prosperity achieved by the west, japan and australia are not the result of monopoly and protectionism but the result of the many developmental and welfare-promoting drivers of the free market economy and free enterprise. in europe this fact has been particularly evident. half our continent has tried a different approach, with central government and large state monopolies. the inefficiency and collapse of monopoly socialism in europe is an experience and a bitter lesson for many european peoples. this is a lesson that we not only should, but also have an obligation to pass on to countries that want to build a democratic welfare society. developing countries must have the same opportunity to develop and create an economic model of free competition and open market economies. it is not the eu's job to try to prevent progress towards prosperous competition. it is not too much market economy that has been the big problem in the developing world. on the contrary, the problem is too little free enterprise and too many monopolies, which have led to mismanagement, corruption and stagnation. i fully share the view that people need clean water, functioning sanitation, basic health care, basic education and access to energy. however, it is not the ownership itself that is important but a high degree of flexibility and efficiency in the provision of these services. competition is of great importance there. the developing countries obviously need a legal framework, and perhaps this is one area where the eu can be of great help to the developing countries. unfortunately, current policy solutions in many developing countries are often ineffective and expensive, reaching only a limited portion of the population. the developing countries that have succeeded in moving from poverty to prosperity and wealth have all prioritized competition and efficiency, not monopoly building and central government.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank georges garot for his valuable contribution to the debate on farmers' incomes. garot is a committed agricultural politician who is knowledgeable and has a positive view of agriculture. i have to say, as a swede, i'm a little jealous. we could have had some of these social democrats in the swedish government; that would have been very useful to us. let me raise a few points which i think are important. garot states that farming must be carried out throughout the union, throughout the territory. it is very important to remember this, especially in the parts of the union where i come from, for example, sweden or finland. he also raises another very important point, namely the difference between cost trends and price trends. there is a gap that can be filled by rationalizations, but not by any means. we must ensure that this gap does not become too large. we have seen the gap grow in my country since joining the eu. it's too big now, and production is falling. we have put forward the agricultural reform; it has been decided. however, it is not good to implement it at different rates in different countries. this is important, as the report also points out. the different pace of implementation of the agricultural reform risks further altering competitiveness or rendering competition neutral in the different countries. it is now of the utmost urgency to introduce rural development and environmental aid as far as possible. there is a risk, as garot says in his report, that this could be adversely affected when the member states are to co-finance. there i feel a worry. however, i must mention some points in this report which i do not think are so good. it's a little reform-negative, if i may say so. the aim is to keep as many people as possible in agriculture. i don't think that's realistic in the long run. i do not think it is realistic to have a far-reaching regulation of the market or politically determined prices, nor a regulated supply of the kind we have with quotas. we must strive to reduce border protection to deal with world trade. as we are now doing this decoupling, it would be best if we could do it completely from the beginning and actually in the whole union at one time. these things will make me and many in the eldr group regrettably vote against. we cannot vote yes to this report, but given all the advantages i mentioned at the beginning, we cannot vote no either. we will probably abstain in the final vote, since it is only one vote.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european union is able to take action against the spread of the disease. with free movement within the union and rapid transmission across borders, a european centre can quickly help mitigate and limit the effects and spread of disease outbreaks. we believe that it is inappropriate to extend the centre's powers to general public health issues. we believe that the commission's proposal is better in some important respects than the european parliament's proposal, which is why we have chosen to support the commission's proposal.
in the final vote i voted in favour of the report, since all the christian-democratic proposals from the ppe-de group were adopted in plenary and have thus considerably improved their content. the fight against poverty is the highest objective of eu development policy. history shows that state monopolies often give rise to economic inefficiency, poor management, corruption and injustice in developing countries. in the case of public services, such as water and energy supply, education and health, the public sector has failed in the majority of these countries. reforms in this sector can thus contribute to improvements and growth. the report makes this clear in a number of amendments tabled by us in the ppe-de group. the report is therefore in line with the commission's assessment that the fight against poverty can only be taken seriously by monitoring objectively all the reform options with a view to boosting economic growth and combating corruption. both public and private monopolies must be replaced by competition and diversity in order to achieve greater equity and better economic governance. clear legal frameworks must guarantee the right to public services. small businesses can be an important factor in the growth of developing countries and should therefore be supported financially and through technical assistance.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr garot very warmly for a good report which gives a very good picture of the agricultural economy today. the economy of agriculture is a very important issue for the development of a vibrant rural area. a vibrant rural area depends entirely on the existence of profitable agricultural holdings. in a europe where 50% of farmers are over the age of 55 and only 8% under the age of 35, a whole new approach to agricultural entrepreneurship is needed. if i were asked today, my advice to a person with a new farm would be to do his financial calculation without the money of society, not to rely on the goodwill of the politicians. there are some points in the report which i cannot support. among other things, it is intended to 'guarantee a reasonable standard of living and stable incomes for the agricultural population in order to maintain agricultural activities throughout the territory of the eu'. another point stresses the importance of maintaining the level of public support for agriculture and the importance of creating conditions that guarantee employment, etc. if you read these sentences to today's farmers, they would not be comfortable with the wording. in a country like sweden, these words do not correspond to reality. only 0.5 percent of the population are farmers today. the restructuring is proceeding at an unprecedented pace. the mid-term review has brought everything to a standstill, except the decommissioning of farms. of course, this cannot be blamed solely on the european community. the swedish government is among those who are most in debt.

commission. on 13 december, the representatives of the member states, meeting at head of state or government level, decided by common agreement on the seat of certain offices and agencies of the european union and, in that context, a decision was taken that the 'european network and information security agency (enisa) shall have its seat in greece, in a location to be determined by the greek government.' due to its specific nature, the commission considers that the agency’s basic requirements include advanced and highly secure information technology equipment, support and operations, sufficiently protected against hacking and security attacks, of which it will certainly be a target. this includes the equipment underpinning normal office tasks, as well as the agency's intranet and databases used to store data necessary to the work of the agency, or database-driven applications and networks required for interaction with any national administrations. furthermore, making information available both to the general public, as well as to the specialised audiences will require a properly designed, hosted, operated and protected website. the very recent malicious code attacks have yet again proven the need for increased coordination between member states to achieve higher levels of security. the commission is convinced that the setting-up of enisa, with its own legal personality, is the most efficient way to achieve this objective. it favours trust and consequently the direct involvement of industry in its work. this involvement is seen as crucial to solving the information security problems in europe. i am pleased to assure you that the commission is committed to launching enisa’s operational activities as soon as possible. concerning the honourable member’s questions on the composition and participation of the management board, the commission, in line with its communication of december 2002 on the operating framework for the european regulatory agencies, generally favours a small-scale management board. i made this clear in a statement during the council on 20 november 2003. however, because of the specific tasks assigned to enisa and the pressing need for its establishment, the commission has not stood in the way of a first-reading agreement between the legislators. the commission has nevertheless pointed out the exceptional nature of the course taken and reaffirms its eagerness to see the general framework applied.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the npt is not just a non-proliferation treaty. it is also the only binding international treaty on nuclear disarmament. nonnuclear powers pledge not to acquire nuclear weapons, and nuclear powers pledge to eliminate theirs. this is reflected in article 6 of the agreement, which commits the parties to serious negotiations for the elimination of all nuclear weapons. this pledge has been signed by all the nuclear powers. at the last five-year summit, the nuclear-weapon states pledged to completely eliminate their nuclear arsenals. in particular, a 13-point action programme for the total denuclearisation of nuclear weapons was adopted unanimously. what the eu must do at the next meeting is, of course, to demand that these commitments be implemented. ireland has long played an active role in the work on nuclear disarmament, including with my own country, sweden. will the council then demand that these commitments be implemented? true, the nuclear powers have reduced their strategic nuclear arsenals. instead, the united states has developed so-called mini-nuclear weapons, designed to be used directly in war, and nuclear weapons that penetrate deep mountains, so-called bunker buster weapons. this is clearly in breach of article 6 of the npt. moreover, the united states has revised its nuclear policy to allow the use of its nuclear weapons, not only in war but also for so-called preventive purposes. this constitutes a serious violation of international law. preventive warfare is a violation of the un charter and a return to the right of the strong to intervene for their own ends, which existed before the united nations was created. it is of course important that the european parliament in its resolution calls on the parties to the npt to live up to their commitments. they must embark on a serious nuclear disarmament and stop any development of new nuclear weapons. how will the commission and the council pursue these demands at the next npt meeting?
the commission's proposal for a regulation on the conservation of fishery resources in the baltic sea is a very good one. the proposal is based on a report by the international council for the exploration of the sea (ices). however, parliament does not want to go so far as to consider the problems less serious. the real message of the amendment from parliament is that the commission is exaggerating the problem of overfishing and that a recovery plan is not necessary. the undersigned chose to vote against the parliament's amendments, as the original commission proposal places greater emphasis on this, taking the problems seriously. in addition, the continued survival of fish stocks is given priority over the socioeconomic effects that any restrictions may have in the worst case.
the european parliament has already adopted a number of amendments to the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. the british exception in this area has unfortunately increasingly become almost the rule, with 33% of workers in the uk having signed an agreement to work more than 48 hours a week. this situation is now tending to spread to several countries, posing a risk to workers' health and undermining safety at work. we christian democrats will not support such a development. it is therefore crucial that i voted in favour of the new wording of paragraph 12, two parts of which were adopted by 275 votes to 229 (nine abstentions) and 335 to 155 (twenty-one abstentions). this means that the time limit for the uk derogation must now be phased out. amendment no 28 means that a long-term solution to the problem of doctors' duty shifts must be addressed before the next consultation phase, which i support, as otherwise it would lead to a demand for some 3,000 new doctors in sweden and an unsustainable funding situation for the landstinget.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i want to be a little personal in this debate because i think that whatever we say here can be perceived as offensive on either side. we can make speeches here in the european parliament that are most likely to satisfy our own constituency back home in britain or in france or, as in my case, in sweden. what contribution are we prepared to make as the european union and the european parliament to the middle east question and to a peaceful future and coexistence between israelis and palestinians? two and a half years ago, i visited nicosia and witnessed the division of that city. i went to belfast when i was 25 and i have seen how that city is divided and how, in recent times, barriers and barriers have been built between the unionists and the republicans, the two groups of the population. in ceuta, spain, in north africa, there is barbed wire to separate the spanish city of ceuta from morocco. the united states has built a barrier between itself and mexico. an electronic border is almost complete between zimbabwe and botswana. am i telling you this to somehow excuse what is happening in the middle east? no, not in any way, but to show that this is found in many other places. in 2000, israel built a un-approved security border with lebanon with electronic and other surveillance using sensors. this was applauded by the european union and by russia, and this was seen as a wise way for israel to unilaterally withdraw its troops from lebanese soil by the year 2000. i hope and believe that the wall and the barrier that has been built is an interim solution or measure. i hope and hope that this is not something permanent, and i hope and believe that the peace talks will be able to resume. i'd like to believe both sides, if possible. the fact that the israeli public has pushed for the construction of this barrier is of course linked to the large number of suicide attacks that have hit the israeli public. 120 suicide bombers have caused nearly 900 deaths and we can speak of up to 6,000 injured israelis. let us, as good europeans, and also as good swedes, try to see this question from both sides. let us hope that we can reach a peaceful solution in the middle east.

the european parliament has previously stated that it regards the turkish presence in northern cyprus as an occupation. the european community was born as a peace project, and our will is to preserve the legacy of de gasperi, schuman, monet and adenauer. in view of this, is it in line with the eu's peace thinking and the legacy of our fathers to give turkey a start date for negotiations on membership of the european union while turkey continues to be an occupying power in northern cyprus? in other words, can you occupy another member state of the european union and at the same time be offered talks about membership?
