madam president, i would like to thank you. the isaf troops and many other aid agencies are doing a great deal of good in afghanistan. however, that work is being sabotaged by the presence of american forces. last year i had the opportunity to visit the us headquarters in bagram with an eu delegation. it became clear that the americans have no plans whatsoever for women's rights, for democracy or for the fight against drugs. they are interested, they say, in any government in afghanistan recognizing the right of u.s. troops to return if they ever leave the country. it's pretty revealing for what it's about. when we were in bagram, there was one thing we weren't allowed to see. an american journalist told us that the prison in bagram makes stories from guantanamo look like the sunday school stories. she had learned that, at any rate, there were several hundred prisoners in bagram prison at the time. they're being tortured. they are not beaten, but they are forced to stand naked for hours, day in and day out, under constant interrogation. eventually, two prisoners died, and she got an honest american doctor to write clearly on the death certificate that the cause of death was murder. it took months before the new york times dared to publish this article. so sensitive is the recognition that the american soldiers in afghanistan, as in many other places, are gangsters who defend american interests by all means, but who completely ignore the local population. let's get rid of the americans and replace them with sensible people from isaf and aid organizations. there are so many others who can make constructive contributions. the americans are only making it worse.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr andré brie, for a very well-written and keen description of the situation in afghanistan and what we are going to do to improve it. i have four points to make to parliament and to commissioner patten. the first is narcotics. as the international community works to combat drug abuse, afghanistan's illicit opium production is adversely affecting the willingness of individual governments in the world to contribute financially to the reconstruction of the country. according to a study by the united nations office on drugs and crime, in 2003 afghanistan accounted for three quarters, or 75 percent, of the world's total production of illicit opium. this is deeply disturbing to say the least. the second point concerns the situation of women. the anti-democratic and islamist taliban regime, when in power, committed the most deliberate violation of women's rights in modern times. the regime imposed a gender apartheid that totally denied women's identity. the taliban regime is now gone, but the view of women in afghanistan is still very much there. the authorities of the country, the international community and the eu must therefore do everything possible to change this view. the third point concerns religious freedom. the first constitution of afghanistan since the fall of the taliban dictatorship raises concerns about certain formulations concerning the dominant role of islamic law, restrictions on certain human rights and individual freedoms and restrictions on women's rights. the last point concerns education. we in the european parliament are calling for an open education system as an alternative to the madrassas, the koran schools, which act as strongholds for muslim extremism and the culture of hatred and where taliban militants are recruited.

mr president, i would like to thank you. when i arrived in brussels this morning, i found that i had received a letter from the world wildlife fund. the letter said that i would soon receive my test results from the blood test i took a few months ago when we could test the chemicals in our blood. i guess a lot of us did this blood test. it is likely that this test will show that i, like everyone else, have a number of completely foreign chemicals in my blood and it is likely that some of them will also be such long-lived organic compounds as we are discussing today. in fact, we know relatively little about what this means for our health and well-being, but we know enough to be concerned. we've had a number of alarming reports about various chemicals causing disease, that they can affect our ability to have children, for example. in view of this, and in particular in view of the fact that the proposal is very welcome, not only the proposal but above all the compromise. in fact, this negotiated compromise is much better than the proposal put forward by the commission. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr frahm, in particular for his work. i would also like to highlight some points which i consider to be crucial improvements. the first is the legal basis. i think it is a very unfortunate trend on the part of the commission to treat environmental issues more and more as internal market issues. from an ecological point of view, this is completely irresponsible. it also reduces the possibilities for member states to introduce more progressive legislation. the union is thus preventing countries which are willing and able to take the lead from doing so, and that is not acceptable. we hope that the commission will take this into account and stick to the legal basis for achieving environmental objectives when it comes to proposals on the environment that this is really about. i also think that the substance of the proposal, the clear prohibition of production, use and marketing, is very important. it is particularly welcome that the ban on hexachlorcyclohexane (hch) including lindane is included in the proposal. this is a reminder that eu chemicals policy is not working in practice we know that there is a very small fraction of the chemicals on the market that are actually tested and that we actually know the effects of. this covers a small part of chemicals policy and now we need a well-functioning, uniform chemicals legislation within the reach system for the registration, assessment and authorisation of chemicals. it is a proposal which is being attacked quite strongly today, but we need to know what these chemicals mean even when they are present in relatively moderate amounts, as is the case with many chemicals. if too many of these chemicals are excluded, we will be left without an effective chemicals policy. i think this work shows that it is possible to take the right decisions on chemicals. this will guide the way forward for the reach review. i would like to thank the rapporteur again.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating mrs pernille frahm and perhaps a little bit of congratulations to ourselves who have participated in these negotiations with the council. we have managed to reach agreement with the irish presidency on this regulation and on the implementation of the stockholm convention on long-lived organic compounds. perhaps not many people thought we would reach an agreement, but pernille frahm has managed this in an extraordinary way. even if all that has been achieved is not ideal and exactly what we want, it is perhaps the best that could be achieved in terms of the political possibilities. i'm very happy. as others have said, it is important that the regulation is now implemented as soon as possible, because persistent organic pollutants are the worst when it comes to hazardous chemical substances. they don't break down, they accumulate in our bodies and throughout the food chain. levels are now so high that they are a threat to both human health and the functioning of ecosystems. the objective must be to eliminate the release of persistent organic pollutants by prohibiting their production, use and import. this means going further, and in some cases much further, than both the stockholm convention and the 1979 protocol on long-range transboundary air pollution. this must also apply to the new member states which will join the eu in may this year. in some cases, exemptions have been requested for, for example, hch and lindane and cannot be accepted under any circumstances. i am often very surprised that it is so difficult for the commission, for the council and perhaps sometimes even for the more conservative part of parliament to get things through which are intended to protect human health and the environment and that it is repeatedly delayed, delayed and stopped. the precautionary principle must be fully applied here, and this also means that new substances must be added to the list of substances prohibited and controlled. as regards the legal basis, i do not understand the commission, nor do i understand that there should be such a discussion. the legal basis must be article 175 of the ec treaty, since these decisions are taken to protect human health and the environment. the whole chemical legislation is aimed at this. this has also been discussed from chester onwards that it is because we cannot protect human health and the environment that we must make good decisions in terms of a comprehensive overall view of chemical legislation. in conclusion, i think that this agreement with the council and the fact that we are getting a first reading is very good, because it is so important that we implement this as soon as possible.
commission. mr president, those members of the house who read the financial times, or indeed the moscow times, will recognise that eu-russia cooperation is not always as straightforward as it could be. i have to say that i regret that deeply. why? because a stable and constructive relationship with russia is essential to the eu, and also because a stable and constructive relationship with the eu is essential to russia. it is clearly in our interest to try to promote close ties with an open, stable and democratic russia, acting, we hope, as a reliable partner which can uphold european values, continue reforms, implement commitments and, in cooperation with the eu, play a constructive role in the newly independent states. the communication which the commission adopted on 9 february, a communication which heavily influenced the report discussed by foreign ministers on monday of this week, took as its starting point the interdependence of the eu and russia and our wish to build a genuine strategic partnership, based on shared values and driven by our evident common interests. there are positive elements to the relationship, but, overall, we have to accept that the results of five years of increasingly intensive cooperation are not as positive as we expected. we have to do something about that. there were three important outcomes from the review of eu-russia relations discussed on monday. first, a strong, clear and public message was passed to russia that we expect the partnership and cooperation agreement to be extended to the ten acceding member states without pre-condition or distinction by 1 may. the commission has held intensive discussions with russia. we hope that we have been able to convince our interlocutors that the question of extending the pca must be rapidly settled. the eu, for its part, is ready, in parallel to the extension of the pca, to discuss any of russia's legitimate concerns over the impact of enlargement. i hope that our attempt to resolve this issue as rapidly as possible will not be affected by the formation of the new government of the russian federation, with which we will look forward to working in due course. second, the ministers confirmed the importance of the eu's relations with russia. it was agreed that the eu should engage with russia, that we should be ready to discuss all complex issues of mutual interest, and that as part of this approach, the eu should stress that its partnership with russia is not only driven by common interests but also founded on shared values. this implies discussing frankly russian practices that run counter to european values, such as human rights in chechnya, media freedom and cooperation on the environment. it also involves not hesitating to defend eu interests vigorously. third, the ministers recognised the need for the eu to change the way in which it does business with russia – to become more 'joined up', to use the bureaucratic terminology. it was agreed that the eu should establish clear, agreed objectives and positions ahead of every meeting with russia. if we wish to build what can honestly be described as a strategic partnership, eu messages must be balanced and coherent, and our ambitious political declarations must be matched by progress on substance. this requires better coordination between policies defined at eu level, and the approach of individual member states to relations with russia. why, for example, is it that the eu subscribes to joint statements with russia, in which we agree to step up cooperation on crisis management, yet the eu is unable to convince russia to work with it to resolve the very real problems in moldova and the southern caucasus? why, in addition, do we see individual member states agree to facilitate visas for russia, at the same time as russia is blocking conclusion of a readmission agreement? many of these views are shared by the report which the honourable member has presented so eloquently this morning; the report is a welcome contribution, in my judgement, to the debate on our relations with russia. our task now is to translate words into action and to establish a more coherent, strategic approach to russia, reflecting the views of the enlarged union. it is important that the eu can make real progress in its work with russia. this matters to business people wishing to gain access to the promising russian market – we should remember, after all, that after enlargement, more than 50% of russia's trade will be with the eu. it also matters to importers of energy, given russia's position as a major supplier of oil and gas. and not least, it matters because, if we are to tackle cross-border problems such as environmental pollution, illegal migration and organised crime, russia will have to shoulder more of the burden than it has previously. discussions at the general affairs and external relations council reaffirmed the interest of the eu in promoting integration in the long term, through the creation of a series of common spaces within the framework of the partnership and cooperation agreement. the commission has advocated strongly that, once agreement is reached on pca extension, the eu should be ready to present a draft joint action plan to russia covering all these four spaces, consistent with our neighbourhood policy and incorporating relevant elements that are of common interest to both the eu and russia. this would help to establish an issues-based agenda, focused on substance, and be a further step towards the strategic partnership that we genuinely want with the russian federation. i would like to thank the honourable member once again for presenting such a thoughtful report and i am sure it will contribute to the outcome that we both wish to see. the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the opposition in abkhazia has intensified its campaign against the leader of the breakaway republic, ardzinba. armenian and the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in my opinion from the committee on employment and social affairs, i have highlighted two factors as being particularly important. firstly, there are the gender pay gaps. they mean that families often choose, for purely economic reasons, to have a maximum working time for the man, i.e. eight hours and perhaps overtime, and part-time for the woman. these wage differences, which are not chosen but dictated by the wage conditions on the market, must be eliminated if families are to be free to choose the family pattern that suits them. the second factor is the hours of work. it is becoming increasingly common for both men and women to work eight hours a day and, in addition, to have long commutes to and from work. in practice, this may result in a working day of 9 to 10 hours for both parties. this pattern imposes an unsustainable burden, especially on families with children. it leads to stress, burnout, sick leave, and problems in family life. i am therefore the only one who can say that we must limit the working time to 12 hours per family per day. it is therefore essential that we divide the working time equally between men and women, i.e. six hours for men and six hours for women. it is much better than parttime for women and fulltime for men, both for the families and for the workplaces. the committee on budgets has already adopted a number of amendments to the proposal, including the one on the reduction of working hours. i would therefore like to repeat this important point: six + six hours is much better than four + eight hours. this is a crucial issue, and one that should be highlighted on international women's day.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is no shortage of good old ideas and no shortage of good new ideas when it comes to combating discrimination against women. there are laws, but there are major shortcomings in implementation and priorities. earlier today we had a press conference in the committee on women's rights and gender equality. i was then told that the number of people being claimed to give priority to this issue. so, while we have legislated and prioritized, the number has increased by 300,000. this clearly shows that the demand side must now be the focus. otherwise we can increase the appropriations for the daphne programme indefinitely without any lasting results. i fully support lissy gröner's proposal for a european year of men's violence against women in 2006. mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by welcoming the commissioner to this important new recruitment. i can hardly see anyone better suited to the task in the present commission. in zambia, several women are murdered by their husbands every week. in the united states, a woman is beaten by her husband every 15 seconds. in france, some 25,000 women are raped each year. this is what i woke up to when my radio woke me up this morning. these were some of the findings of amnesty international's report on the appalling situation of women worldwide. i'm not a moralist, but there is a disgusting limit to all the pornography that bubbles up in our homes, on the internet, in newspapers and advertisements, in hotels. when you're a parent of two kids, it's hard to defend yourself. the limit is being reached. there is a clear link between prostitution and the increasingly graphic pornography on the one hand and the oppression and violence against women on the other. within the eu, half a million women and children are sold every year. according to lissy gröner, the figure is perhaps 800,000 women and children. this is shameful, extremely shameful. the lisbon process is certainly needed to strengthen the position of women in the labour market, there is no doubt about that, but if europe is to be a decent continent, this abominable crossborder trade must be stopped. my appeal to you, madam commissioner, and to the president, pat cox, is to take up this issue at the spring meeting in brussels as the first item. on 1 may the bells of freedom will ring in europe, not those of female slavery.
mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i'm a feminist, you know. for me, feminism is about seeing and recognizing that women often have a subordinate role in society and being willing to do something about it. in europe, what the eu does to make society more equal matters very much. i remember very well what happened when ingvar carlsson, as the new prime minister of sweden in 1994, appointed his government, and half of them were women. after that government was formed, it became impossible for the political parties in sweden to do anything other than to ensure that more women were given heavy political responsibilities. for us social democrats, it was a matter of course that every other woman was in the political assemblies. the same must happen in the eu. women and men should be equal. the new parliament will be elected on the basis of equality between men and women. when a new commission is appointed, it must also be gender-balanced. the same applies to all parts of the european union. there are two main reasons. firstly, women and men must of course be treated equally. we have the same rights and obligations. secondly, the signals that the eu sends are of great importance. equal opportunities for women and men is one such signal that must be sent from us here to all actors in society.
madam president, i would like to thank you. this is a very important report for women, for women's right to their own bodies and for women's right to reproductive health. first, some facts. we now know that every minute a woman dies from pregnancy-related causes. every minute, 100 abortions are performed, and nearly half of them are performed by people without medical training. every year 70,000 women die as a result of unsafe abortions. every minute, ten people are infected with hiv. every year, two million girls between the ages of five and 15 are trafficked into the commercial sex trade, into modern slavery with no contraceptive protection. rape and sexual violence are also on the rise in europe. in many countries, female genital mutilation is still being inflicted on girls and women, and this is unacceptable. such a violation can result in serious injury and even death. one in three women has been a victim of violence, according to a recent amnesty report. in developing countries, reproductive problems are the leading cause of death and disability for women of childbearing age. in afghanistan, half of all deaths among women aged 15 to 50 are pregnancy-related. to refuse to support organizations that provide contraceptive advice is to knowingly contribute to the unsafe abortions and premature deaths of hundreds of thousands of women and to the infection of hundreds of thousands of women with hiv each year. this is a serious violation of human rights and poses a serious risk to women's reproductive and mental health. the eu and the member states must of course fulfil their obligations and finance and implement the cairo action programme. the eu must integrate sexual and reproductive health into development policy and, of course, require recipient countries to ban fgm.

- the next item is the recommendation for second reading (a5-0090/2004) by the committee on civil liberties and rights and justice and home affairs, on the common position of the council with a view to the adoption of a directive of the european parliament and of the council on the right of citizens of the union and their family members to move and reside freely within the territory of the member states, amending regulation (ec) no 1612/68 and repealing directives 64/221/eec, 68/360/eec, 72/194/eec, 73/148/eec, 75/34/eec, 75/35/eec, 90/364/eec, 90/365/eec and 93/96/eec (13263/33/2003 - c5-0014/2004 - 2001/0111 (cod)) (rapporteur: mr santini).
commission. - madam president, i will speak briefly to thank all the speakers for their support for the adoption of the common position. it seems to me particularly important that we can stand in the european parliament elections with the actual proof that the fourth freedom of the treaties, freedom of movement, has been revived thanks to this legislative initiative and thanks to the adoption of this directive by the parliament and the council. of course, as you all know, the commission had bigger ambitions than the text on the table today. but we must recognize that this compromise is a really acceptable compromise. as regards the concept of family, i would draw your attention to the fact that the directive recognises the freedom of movement of registered partnerships and that article 3 provides for a clause to facilitate the freedom of movement of long-term relationships. it is true that there is room for interpretation, but we cannot say that we have ignored a social reality. given that the union has no competence to legislate on family law, we are dealing with a very variable european legal framework as regards the concept of family itself. in the field of expulsions, it is true that we would like to have a stronger guarantee against expulsions. but if we have not obtained the solid framework that the commission wanted in its proposal, at least we must recognize that the rules in the common position give a wide protection, because the guarantee against expulsion becomes wider and deeper as the stay in the host country is extended over time. i therefore believe that we have also achieved an improvement on the present situation. finally, as regards the question of enlargement, i shall not enter into the debate on the restrictive measures which are being adopted. the conformity of these measures with the accession treaty and the community acquis as a whole will have to be verified. however, these restrictive measures are taken, as i understand it, on the basis of the accession treaties which provide for an initial period of two years during which derogations from the general rules on access to the labour market will be possible. the directive is therefore primarily a directive whose objective is to guarantee freedom of movement for all citizens of the 25 member states beyond this transitional period. i therefore think that the message we are sending by adopting this directive, which everyone will be able to benefit fully from in the not too distant future, is positive, even for newcomers.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i want to raise a point of order. i would like to make a complaint about the way we are handling the legislative procedure in the european parliament. i tabled four amendments which were voted down in committee. this is perfectly acceptable and i also have a reservation, but the deadline for amendments to this part-session expired at 7 p.m. this evening. several of the colleagues who could have thought of signing these amendments have not come here in time because of the problems we always have when we have to go to strasbourg. i think that the deadline could have been extended to tomorrow evening or tomorrow afternoon, so that we would have had an opportunity to deal with this. i do not think this is acceptable and i hope that you can ensure that we have a better order of things in the future.
- i understand your views on the time limit, but as you know, it is in accordance with the rules of procedure. it has to do with administration and translations, but we take your views to heart. this meeting is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon.
- the next item is the report (a5-0095/2004) by mr oostlander, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the draft council framework decision establishing minimum rules on the content of offences relating to illicit drug trafficking and the penalties for such offences (15102/2/2003 - c5-0618/2003 - 2001/0114 (cns)).
madam president, i would like to thank you. oostlander and i share a similar view of the terrible ravages of drugs in europe. many politicians seem to have given up on the so-called 'light drugs'; i wonder what robert evans really meant. many colleagues in this house are advocating a devious policy which, in my view, will only lead to more drug addicts, more crime and more space for the giant drug syndicates. a clear and consistent policy is the only thing that will help. abusers will be treated, but the abuse must be punished. it is a pipe dream to think that a little drug liberalism will keep young people away from harder drugs. it is true that this directive is a step in the right direction, but at the same time we must remember that the 14 have had to make considerable concessions to the netherlands, where coffee shops, as oostlander says, are allowed to continue to ravage. the drug tourism to amsterdam can continue and the netherlands can continue to export drug addiction to other countries. this is unacceptable. we must have free movement, but that freedom must not be used for drugs and crime.
madam president, i would like to thank you. many of us in the european parliament have waited a long time to see concrete action to combat drug trafficking. the framework decision has been negotiated for a long time, and that is welcome and important. it has also highlighted the political, cultural and legal differences which characterize the union. i am sure that we can sometimes be satisfied with the little things, but i share olle schmidt's view on this matter. the attitude of some countries to personal use of drugs is a little difficult for us to understand. the compromise means that personal consumption is not included in the scope. in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, member states which are still in a position to do so may impose penalties. it's not a requirement, but it's possible and it should be. no national legislation is threatened by this. swedish law prohibits the use of drugs. that's good, and that's the way it should stay. no green or other proposals should be made to regulate swedish drug policy. that is how the overwhelming majority of my people view the green idea of allowing drugs in certain amounts. this is simply the wrong way to go. it's giving up. instead, we moderates advocate effective, individualized detox programs. the framework decision must be combined with other measures, such as the closure of drug production in afghanistan, where we should do more, and also measures on border, police and customs cooperation within the eu.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. it is obviously difficult to try to reach an agreement at first reading. it takes will, knowledge and cooperation. i would like to thank all the members of this parliament who have contributed to this process by resisting and supporting it. it's been hard, but it's been very, very constructive. i shall now turn to some of the aspects which are most important to me in this context. this is the case, for example, with the scope. i am exceptionally pleased that animal health and welfare are now covered by the inspections. it is perhaps the greatest success of parliament in this extremely sensitive area, which is of the utmost importance to all our voters in all countries. this applies, for example, to the transport of animals across europe, and it is important that they are at least included in the control programme. parliament also inspected the labelling during the inspections. the control programmes of the countries concerned shall include the inspection of the correctness of the labelling of the food packaging, its compliance with the rules and the de facto conformity of the contents and the declaration of contents. this is a key point for us as normal consumers when we are going to buy a quick dinner on a normal thursday afternoon. the whole chain should be covered from the field to the shop, cafeteria and restaurant is the principle which is of course pervasive throughout food safety legislation. another aspect, which i am both disappointed and happy about, is transparency. on the committee's part, a clear majority called for a system of name, shame and fame. it was not possible to do so because the member states invoked legal, constitutional grounds. as a liberal, i may have come to realize that it is not the job of government to say that one producer is good and the other is bad. with the transparency system now in this regulation, we have left this function of name, shame and fame to civil society. a consumer group, an animal welfare group or a local media outlet can request to receive the inspection reports and thus provide this function and tell citizens what is going on behind the scenes. the financing of the levy is a very controversial issue - not between the commission, the council and myself, but within parliament. some want a fixed, flat fee, one that is firm and equal for all. that might be very good, but this has been tried and failed. not all countries in the union are exactly alike. you do not have the same conditions from portugal to finland or from ireland to hungary. however, i think that the proposal before us is actually quite balanced. fees are to be charged, but the expenditure to be included is clearly defined. in addition, special attention should be paid to small and traditional enterprises, which i think is very important. it is important that we in the european parliament take care of the small producers, the unusual products, those which are the essence of europe's cultural heritage on the table. we have to defend them and we have to give them a chance. they are not exempt from security concerns, but they may in fact be given less fees for this check. i appeal to all of you to vote today despite all the problems that exist, and to follow my compromise list. in conclusion, i would like to thank once again the commission, the council and, in particular, the italian and irish presidencies. thank you so much for a wonderful collaboration!
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i think we should vote now. we have had the opportunity to discuss and i have had close contact with the shadow rapporteurs and the groups which have been interested in contact.
in writing. - (sv) we have voted against the compromise on the harmonization of the laws of the member states on intellectual property rights, primarily because of the amendment to article 9. if the proposals to amend article 9 are adopted, it would be contrary to swedish case law, first and foremost the principle of free assessment of evidence. if such extensive changes are to be made to legislation and thus to swedish case law, we believe that the changes must be well thought out and worked through and not rushed through as is happening now. we are not therefore opposed to a change in legislation, but we do not think that the changes in their present form are satisfactory.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken the initiative in the form of a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. we support the council's decision in its entirety and believe that this amendment limits the scope of the framework decision and therefore clearly undermines the council's text. we also want to stress that the union should adopt every means to combat the use and sale of drugs. we interpret the council decision as a ban on the drug trade and therefore believe that so-called coffee shops should not exist in the eu.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) drug policy is not harmonised in the eu today, but each country draws up its own policy. this does not prevent drug problems from being high on the political agenda. abuse is occurring at a younger and younger age, and more and more youths see no danger in trying marijuana. even the so-called adhesives, that is to say the most dangerous synthetic substances, are becoming more and more widespread. in a europe without frontiers we risk having even greater drug problems as drug legalization spreads outside the netherlands. it seems that many have already given up the fight for a drug-free europe. it is worth repeating that there is a link between the number of people who test and the number of people who are at risk of becoming addicted. that is why the possession and use of drugs must remain a criminal offence. the wrong signals are still being sent to europe's young people when anyone can buy 5 grams of cannabis in a so-called coffee shop. we must not be satisfied with this framework decision but must continue to strive for a drug-free europe. we say no. we do not want to legalise, we do not want to liberalise, and we have not given up the dream of zero tolerance for drugs.
in writing. - (sv) we are in favour of an agency for better coordination and cooperation in the field of border control. we would, however, like to stress the importance of national self-determination in the implementation of border controls. the powers of the member states must not be restricted and their influence on the development of crossborder cooperation must be preserved intact.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the right to vote is guaranteed. we therefore welcome a framework directive in this area which sets targets for, for example, working time, childcare and minimum levels of parental leave. we do not think, however, that the organization of welfare should be decided at eu level. we have therefore voted against formulations which call for special tax breaks, the use of so-called checks or otherwise regulate welfare policy in detail. we also want to emphasize that a family can be made up of different forms of coexistence and therefore support the wording which establishes this. even families which do not consist of a father, a mother and a child must also be recognised and have the same legal status as the traditional family.
in the case of the family, the family is not a political issue. it must be possible for both parents to combine work and family responsibilities. the moderate family policy will have a very flexible childcare system. we can in principle support several of the proposals in the report. we note that the explanatory statement of the report emphasizes that the eu has no competence in the field of family policy. we agree with that and have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. - (sv) what is it? i have strongly supported the european parliament's statement in paragraph 3 that it is christian-democratic in its view that family policy should create the conditions for parents to spend more time with their children. the same paragraph also emphasizes the value of this also "having positive effects in terms of establishing families and ensuring more stable families". in view of this, as stated in paragraph 2, it is important to 'address the demographic problem facing the majority of member states'. in line with our social vision, recital k of the report states that 'the rights of the child should be at the heart of family policies'. the ppe group succeeded in having recital g rejected (321 votes against, 169 in favour and 21 against) and thus attempted to create a new and diffuse definition of what a family is. that is certainly not a task for the eu. the ppe group believes that the concept of the family is based on the right of children to a mother and a father and on the responsibility of parents for their children. children must always be at the heart of the shaping of our society, while the family is the most important and fundamental building block of society. that is why i voted for the report as a whole (424 votes in favour, 51 against and 37 abstentions).
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) before the swedish referendum on eu membership in 1994, all parties working for eu membership stated that the eu had no competence to take decisions on issues of cohabitation, marriage law and abortion. they promised to work towards keeping these issues strictly within the national sphere of legislation, without any eu interference. we stand by this promise to the swedish electorate. we believe that national legislation is much better in this area than common eu legislation. we can see that other swedish parties have abandoned this promise and are ready to bring these issues up at eu level. this is detrimental to swedish citizens and risks being contrary to swedish practice and custom. there is no immediate need for coordination and the best adapted legislation is obtained if the various national parliaments in the eu prepare legislation in this area. the eu's aid policy must also respect the laws of each country. we share the swedish view on the right to abortion, but in respect for third countries we have voted against the parts of the junker report which call for free abortion. the report also focuses on the serious health problems in developing countries, where eu aid policy plays an important role.
in writing. - (sv) i, together with the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats, voted against the report as a whole, which was nevertheless adopted (287 votes in favour, 196 against and 13 abstentions). we want population, aid and development policies to combine the fight for democracy and human rights with respect for the democratically decided legislation of each country, in this case family and health policies. the report does not express such a desire or view. an example of this is paragraph 20, which contains a requirement to "provide safe abortion". we demanded a clarification of this point in amendment no 20 with the addition of 'in the countries where it is legal'. amendments nos 21 and 30, which i supported, also sought greater respect for the laws of other countries. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. the eu and the un are thus showing a lack of respect for the laws of other countries. this undermines confidence in aid work, creates conflict, clashes of cultures, religions and values and leaves a bitter aftertaste of european superiority.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i should like to congratulate the united kingdom on the fact that today it is to take home five of the british citizens who have been held at guantánamo bay. according to swedish media reports today, they will be arrested and interrogated when they land in the uk tonight. four more british detainees at guantanamo will not be released, at least not yet. the swedish prisoner, mehdi ghezali, is also still in custody and there is no prospect of his release. it's been like this for over two years. as regards the swedish citizen, the swedish government initially indicated that it would raise the matter with its colleagues in the council of the european union. here we are today, over two years later, and we've only gotten this far. mehdi ghezali is young, like so many of these prisoners. these are young people, and many of them can even be considered children. mehdi ghezalis's family hasn't heard from him in 13 months. the swedish foreign ministry officials who have been allowed to visit mehdi ghezali are not allowed to tell the family how he is doing. his lawyer hasn't been allowed to see him either. commissioner patten said that we should have a good relationship with the united states. we shall have good relations with the united states, but we shall also have good relations with all other nations. here i think the old saying "it takes two to tango" is good. the us attitude on this issue does not, unfortunately, encourage any greater confidence. of course we remember 9/11, but i also remember the images that have emerged from guantanamo bay, after all, of young people sitting in cages, handcuffed. we're committed to human rights. we can't just have a debate about them on a regular basis. they must also mean something, and that is why i would like to thank ole andreasen for a very good report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. now there are only fifty days left on the long journey that began with the joyful scenes of november 9, 1989, at the brandenburg gate in berlin. in fifty days in writing. - (sv) we are anxious for the directive on the right of citizens of the union and their family members to move and reside freely within the territory of the member states to enter into force as soon as possible. we therefore support the decision reached by the council.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken the initiative in the form of a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. we are concerned that country after country is introducing transitional rules for the new member states. we now risk having to live with these transitional rules which discriminate against the new member states for the next 10 to 20 years. the transitional rules are being introduced on the grounds that there is a risk of misuse of the support schemes in the current member states when the borders are opened to the new eu citizens in the spring. today, a 10-hour workweek is enough to gain access to the new country's welfare system. instead, the requirement should be that you can make a living from your work. we have therefore tried to get some general measures adopted, covering all eu citizens, whereby the definition of work is changed so that work is to be paid for. this already applies to pensioners and students. in this way the eu is opened to the free movement of labour, but not to the abuse of subsidies. we also have doubts about the definition of family member, which should be extended to include spouses, registered partners and unmarried partners of any sex. since we are not getting support for this, we shall vote against the report.
in writing. - (sv) we have chosen neither to vote for nor against the prets, pack and ilvari reports. the reason for this is that we believe that issues of culture and education should be at national level, where decisions are taken as close as possible to the citizens. we are opposed in principle to the pack report selecting a small number of schools which receive a large proportion of the total training aid from the eu. we think there are many other good schools that should also be taken into account. if the eu is to become more effective and more active on the major crossborder issues, it also requires that we leave the policy on everyday issues such as education, culture and youth to the member states themselves. these questions must be brought closer to the citizens!
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) we voted against all the amendments to the commission's proposal and against the report as a whole. we want a modern european agriculture in line with the reform of the common agricultural policy. we believe that the commission's proposal is a step forward in achieving the objectives of the union's agricultural policy. we are in favour of a fundamental reform of the agricultural policy, with the decoupling of agricultural aid as the guiding principle. that is why we support the commission's proposal. supporting the wording of the report would be a major step backwards in the work of reforming european agriculture, since the wording of parliament continues to promote direct aid and production-related aid to an all too great extent. the eu's agricultural policy aims to give farmers greater freedom to produce what the market demands, leading to a consumer-friendly supply. this is important, especially from an international perspective, in order not to make it more difficult for developing countries to compete on the european market with their agricultural products. these are fundamental principles which are important to us and which must be taken into account in the union's agricultural policy, particularly in the cotton and tobacco sectors.
in writing. - (sv) we moderates believe that direct support for tobacco, olives, cotton and hops should be abolished. european support for tobacco cultivation is neither morally nor economically justifiable. we also believe that the protective duty on olives should be abolished.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) i voted against all the amendments and against the final vote on the report because we think it is wrong for the eu to continue to give more aid to tobacco plantations, olive growing and cotton production than the commission's original proposal entails. we believe that this aid should be phased out and that taxpayers' money should be used in a more appropriate way. the ppe-de group sees increased support as a problem of principle and, in the case of tobacco, there are strong ethical arguments against the aid itself. since many developing countries are totally dependent on their cotton production, eu aid distorts competition. these countries are even less well placed than regions in the eu to deal with the transition problems that arise if certain parts of production are phased out.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) we voted against amendments nos 1-28, 30-39 and 40-43 to the commission proposal and against the report as a whole. we want a modern european agriculture in line with the reform of the common agricultural policy. we believe that the commission's proposal is a step forward in achieving the objectives of the union's agricultural policy. we are in favour of a fundamental reform of the agricultural policy, with the decoupling of agricultural aid as the guiding principle. that is why we support the commission's proposal. supporting the wording of the parliament's report would be a major step backwards in the work of reforming european agriculture, since the wording of the parliament continues to promote direct aid and production-related aid to an excessively large extent. the eu's agricultural policy aims to give farmers greater freedom to produce what the market demands, leading to a consumer-friendly supply. this is important, especially from an international perspective, in order not to make it more difficult for developing countries to compete on the european market with their agricultural products. these are fundamental principles which are important to us and which must be taken into account in the union's agricultural policy.
(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) i voted against all the amendments and against the final vote on the report because we believe that it is wrong for the eu to continue to give more support to olive growing than the commission's original proposal entails. we believe that this aid should be phased out and that taxpayers' money should be used in a more appropriate way. the ppe-de group sees increased support as a problem of principle. since many developing countries are totally dependent on their cotton production, eu aid distorts competition. these countries are even less well placed than regions in the eu to deal with the transition problems that arise if certain parts of production are phased out. the parliament's proposal not only involves higher aid than the commission's proposal, but also imposes more specific requirements on products and classification systems. this may be commendable, but it is often a way of protecting oneself against external competition, that is, a hidden obstacle to trade which makes free trade difficult.
in writing. - (sv) we moderates support the demand that the guantanamo prisoners should have a fair trial and we have therefore voted in favour of this report. we do not, however, consider it self-evident that the prisoners meet the conditions of the geneva convention for being considered prisoners of war, and it is therefore not correct in the report to invoke the provisions of the geneva convention on the treatment of prisoners of war as being synonymous with the rights of the prisoners of guantánamo.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? so here we are again, and i am making a new attempt to make this house aware of the importance of changing the current rules on vat collection by postal services. the committee and the plenary rejected my proposal a few months ago, so now lulling and i are returning with a number of amendments to appease the critical colleagues. i myself, as the commissioner says, would have preferred the commission's proposal straight away, but there was no majority in favour. the market is being liberalised at european level as well as at national level. several former state-owned postal and telephone companies have been converted into public limited companies. they need different rules. the current unfair tax treatment not only hinders the development of open markets and thus the liberalisation of postal services but also distorts and hinders competition for both public and private operators. the economic impact on consumers would be negligible. it's the famous one-penny-for-a-stamp raise, or no raise at all. there may even be a price reduction if member states use a reduced rate of taxation. the postal operators mentioned by the commissioner pay vat. they can also deduct input vat. this way they can reduce their overall costs. this is good for new investments and the modernisation of old monopolies. i'd like to do it again. we've made some modifications since the last treatment. the weight limit for the reduced rate of taxation for standard postal items is proposed to be raised from 2 to 10 kg. this is one of the weight limits set by the upu. in this way, important parts of the standard mail would be included in the weight class for the reduced rate. these are daily newspapers, weekly newspapers and other parcel items. the entry into force of the directive is postponed to 2007. it will give member states and postal operators sufficient time to adapt to the new provisions of the directive. it also fits in with the various deregulation steps planned for the postal market. it is also proposed that member states which applied a super-reduced rate of taxation on 1 january 2003 may continue to apply this rate to postal services. we also have proposals for various organizations that are concerned with such things as gifts and other things. i hope that these compromises will be sufficient. it would be unfortunate if nothing happened at all. the big question that has been dealt with recently is what happens to the royal mail. the other postal companies, as the commissioner says, support this proposal. the industry thinks it's good. data from the uk national regulator postcom shows that the abolition of the vat exemption for public service operators would not result in price increases for consumers. instead, it would create a level playing field which would ultimately lead to increased competition, greater choice for postal customers and lower prices. i spoke to a senior official at postcom who put it this way: there is no reason for royal mail to retain this vat exemption. it's not necessary to protect the royal mail. it is not necessary to protect consumers. it's the opposite. i think it is a pity that so many clever colleagues like goebbels, skinner, villiers and, not least, my own party colleague huhne, have not been able to persuade in a reasonable direction. i regret this and can only say that if parliament says no again tomorrow, which unfortunately is very encouraging, i will have failed miserably. we can perhaps live with that, i can live with that, but the european consumers have lost. they're the big losers. i hope my colleagues can think about that, if only during the night before the vote tomorrow.

(parliament adopted the legislative resolution) during the swedish referendum on eu membership in 1994, all political parties working for swedish membership claimed that cohabitation, marriage law and abortion issues were outside the eu's decision-making competence. all parties promised to work towards keeping these issues strictly within the national sphere of legislation, without eu interference. we moderates are committed to this promise to the swedish electorate and we believe that national legislation is much better than common eu legislation in this area. we can see that other swedish political parties have abandoned their promise and are now ready to take the issues to the eu level. in the long run, this is to the disadvantage of swedish citizens and there is a risk that it will be in contrast to the traditions and customs of sweden. there is no apparent need for coordination. the best adapted legislation is to be found if the various national parliaments in the eu themselves draft the legislation. in view of the enlargement of the european union, the legislation of individual acceding countries in this area must also be respected. we swedish moderates support the swedish view on the right to abortion and homosexual partnerships, but out of respect for other eu countries we voted against the section in the brok report which calls for a new abortion and partnership legislation in poland.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the health and social care systems are properly integrated into the community. since the report sets out in great detail how the member states are to organise their health and care services for the elderly, we have voted against it. we have done so despite the fact that we can in principle support some of the proposals that are presented in the report, such as the right of eu citizens to health care throughout the union.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the development of the community's economy. in her report, karin jöns deals with health and care for the elderly, which affect the individual in a variety of ways and to a large extent in their daily lives. she is addressing many important health aspects, which we also believe are of great importance to the people of europe. however, we believe that the responsibility for health care should lie as close to the user as possible. in order to have the best tailored health care, we believe that the responsibility should be at national level and not at eu level. that is why we have voted against this report.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i may have a certain ability to look like a very gorgeous mother. i am very much out in the world and i can verify that there is genuine anxiety and fear among our constituents - the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we have a joint debate here tonight on all three pillars of the aarhus convention. i welcome that debate and i also welcome the commission's proposal for a directive. by signing the aarhus convention, the community has shown its willingness to improve environmental legislation and also to involve the public in environmental decision-making in a completely new way. a future ratification would effectively oblige the community to ensure that citizens and ngos can enjoy this right in accordance with community law, which is very good. although the community and the member states signed the convention in 1998 and it entered into force in 2001, only a few eu member states have ratified it. it is time for the public and ngos to have access to justice in environmental matters and, of course, to information and participation in the decision-making process, as discussed in the korholab report. this will be of great importance in making environmental policy much more effective in the eu. environmental rules will be better administered and enforced, implementation will be faster and environmental management will be better managed. how can you say that so gallantly? indeed, there are studies showing that environmental legislation has become much stricter in countries where there has been the possibility of bringing environmental cases and testing the application of environmental legislation. there's a direct connection here. the aim of agenda 21, which is also the guiding principle of the commission, is to make the environment the property of the individual, not the public authorities. for the first time, environmental legislation is being linked to human rights, and i think that is a good development. we also have a responsibility to future generations. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection voted in favour of the directive as a minimum framework, allowing member states to go further and have more extensive access to justice. i think that's an excellent suggestion. an environmental issue must apply both to the administration of the authorities and to criminal law, because the aarhus convention does not distinguish between civil and criminal proceedings, and i do not really understand why the commission would want to make that distinction here. we do not agree on some points, and this is clear from the amendments to this directive. i think, for example, that eu legislation should not introduce new and unnecessary restrictions such as those on recognised associations, qualified entities. it would force countries which today have a very generous outlook to impose restrictions. i cannot therefore accept the amendments tabled by the ppe-de group to which mr korhola referred. this applies in particular to articles 8 and 9 and sets out new criteria and procedures which are not at all in the aarhus convention. i think that would create new obstacles, and i do not see why we should have to do that. nor do i understand why geographical and temporal limitations or restrictions, which are also provided for in articles 8 and 5, should be adopted in relation to the competence of the parties. both the commission proposal and amendment no 32 by the ppe-de group, for example, require that one must be active in a given area in order to have access to documents or to a court. i think this is completely wrong, because environmental issues look very different today. a small local environmental organisation, for example, might need expert help from an international organisation, so i think this is restrictive. i think that it is enough to be an independent, non-profit-making legal person to be able to bring an action for the protection of the environment and health. it is also very important that the definition of environmental legislation should cover all policy areas and not just article 175, to which this proposal refers. all environmental legislation relating to article 95 should of course also be eligible. finally, i would just like to mention something about the costs. i have put forward proposals that costs should not be an obstacle to bringing legal proceedings. the public must be given the help and support they need. in the case of actions in the public interest, the cost should not be an obstacle. you have to be very generous here. i therefore hope that the members will vote in favour of the proposals i have examined, so that we can achieve good legislation which does not create new obstacles but, on the contrary, does what we want to do, namely improve environmental legislation in the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the aarhus convention is of great importance. in practice, it strengthens the capacity of citizens to have both visibility and effective influence on decisions on environmental issues that directly affect them. i should like to begin by commenting on the part of the implementation which concerns the schörling report on judicial review in environmental matters. a proposal has been put forward to reject the report in its present form, and mr thors has just spoken of this. i myself have some difficulty in understanding the reasons for rejecting this report. the european union is a signatory to this convention. it should then reasonably have an obligation to ensure, through its regulatory framework, that the convention is also implemented. i suppose that's a reasonable consequence of signing it. i believe that this will be particularly important when the union is enlarged by ten new members. it is important to involve the new member states so that they can achieve with us what is provided for in the convention itself. it is also quite clear from the report that it does not prevent any country from having more progressive legislation. each country is free to go further, to extend the rights that exist in other areas. the committee makes this clear in amendments nos 4 and 5. the point is that the report is concerned with the environment. this is perhaps the part of eu law that is least respected, given the number of cases before the court. i think that the proposal adopted in committee could be improved on a few points. first, it should be extended to all decisions having an impact on the environment, not just those taken on the basis of article 175 of the treaty. secondly, it is important not to specify in detail how the concept of environmental organisation is to be defined. it can hardly be the eu's job to do that. it is also important not to provide for any limitation as to who may bring an action. i shall devote the last few seconds of my speaking time to the korhola report. it is important that as much legislation as possible be covered by this report. it is important that institutions such as the european investment bank, which is a major financial actor, should also be covered by the scope of application, so that they must comply with the obligations laid down in the report. i also think it is very important to give the public a fair hearing so that they are covered by this framework.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to begin by thanking and congratulating you on the result that has been achieved. i know that it was very difficult to get this very important directive into effect. at any rate, we now have a proposal to be voted on tomorrow, and i and the verts/ale group will be voting in favour of it. that doesn't mean we're completely satisfied with the outcome. the polluter pays principle is excellent. it is also good that we should have had a harmonised system of liability to prevent environmental damage and to ensure that damage that has already occurred is remedied. as toine manders said, europe was not quite ready for it. parts of europe, at least, may not have been ready for it. there were quite a few of us who wanted a more harmonized system, and we could have gone a little further than we have now. after the first treatment, we were very satisfied, but after the second treatment, we were not so satisfied anymore. this is a compromise we have to live with, and it is good that something has been achieved. the proposal that the commission should report after six years on the compulsory financial security systems is extremely important for the functioning of this system. it is a way of achieving a compromise at the final stage a statement by the commission making it clear that it intends to fulfil these obligations under the compromise between parliament and the council. i am not sure that such a statement is binding, but i hope it is so that we do not drag out the time over these six years. i hope that is true. perhaps margot wallström could say something about the legal status of this type of statement.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. before going into the details of this proposal and of parliament's proposals for changes and improvements, i would like to recall the background to this directive. the triggering factors were the two major environmental disasters in spain and romania, which the commissioner also mentioned. we should know that the problem of collapsing mine dams is something that occurs almost every year in different parts of the world, including the eu. at the same time, we can say that there is a stealthy environmental disaster in progress, which consists of old mining waste, of which we often know very little, leaking into nature, especially heavy metals. overall, this is perhaps a much bigger environmental problem than the mining that is currently taking place. at the same time, eu legislation in this area has not worked well, because the existing waste directives, which today partly concern mining waste, are not working and are not adapted to this type of industrial production. it is therefore clear that a new directive is needed in this area. we would therefore like to thank the commission for the proposal. it has many advantages, for example in terms of dust safety and in terms of effectively banning the high cyanide method, as was the case in romania. that method will be made impossible in the future by this directive. i would also like to see more improvements from the committee on the environment, public health and consumer affairs and as rapporteur. firstly, it is important that this covers all relevant types of waste. we have therefore chosen to remove some exemptions, for example for waste from exploration. i also think it is important that it covers waste that is shipped away from the nearest place of production. i am particularly grateful that we have been able to agree on a definition which i think works well, because the question of definition has been a bit complicated in this case. there is now agreement within the political groups. i would also like to mention the inert non-hazardous waste. it is important to recognize that even waste that is not hazardous in itself and in chemical terms can cause major environmental problems. it can do so if it is stored improperly, if it is allowed to destroy water resources, or if soils are fertilized. it is important that this waste also be covered by rules requiring its recovery and proper management. here i think that the decision taken by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer affairs does not go far enough, and i have therefore tabled amendment no 98 which would correct this as i see it. one of the most important points in this report concerns historical waste, which is perhaps the greatest environmental problem of all. it is not enough to simply take stock, you must also make sure that you actually do something about it, otherwise you are only halfway to doing something about the environmental problems. i think it is important to link this to the proposals in the report to also be able to use funds from the structural funds to really address this. it will be an opportunity, not least for the new member states, to tackle these rather significant environmental problems. i also think that this should be seen as an opportunity for new technologies and for the research and development that exists in the eu today and that in part already exists and that, above all, can become world leaders in this field. as regards the financial guarantees from the companies, we have again chosen to make a compromise between the political groups before the first reading. this means that we are leaving a great deal of national freedom to design these economic guarantees. this is good, since systems already exist in many countries. at the same time, this must be approved by the commission so that the guarantees meet the requirements, which is reasonable. as regards the technical solutions to the various regulations contained in the proposal, my aim as rapporteur has been not to go into technical details and to lock these proposals in various technical solutions. this would impede adaptation to technological progress. i too think that is the result we have achieved. another point i would like to make is that much of the most irresponsible mining globally is taking place in the poorest countries. we have received a letter in the report stating that, in so far as eu funding is used, including through the european investment bank, for example, the requirements of the directive must be met as a condition for support for mining projects. another point i have chosen to emphasize is the monitoring of the dams. even if design requirements are set, the shortcomings often consist in the fact that the ponds are not adapted later and that they are not monitored as needed if the production conditions change. we have seen that in these accidents. another important point is that we want planning for the closure phase to be done from the outset. on the whole, i think that the proposal from the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection is a significant reinforcement of the commission's work, but it is also fully in line with its work. i hope that through these proposals we can tackle the basic problems i mentioned at the beginning: avoiding the big dams, not including the environmental toxins that are leaking into nature today and getting legislation in this area that actually works and is comprehensive. the conditions for that are now in place. i would like to take two seconds to thank the shadow rapporteurs and the commission's heads of department for their good cooperation.
